# Snapback 4 — agent command sheet

Read this, then [LLP 3000](../llp/3000-snapback4.explainer.md) (the root and
the bakeoff protocol), [LLP 2000.000](../llp/2000.000-keep-the-core-flip-two-substrates.rfc.md)
(the design brief), [`NOT-DOING.md`](./NOT-DOING.md), and the tail of
[`JOURNAL.md`](./JOURNAL.md). `rules/RULES.md` at the repository root is
adopted here as written; LLPs for Snapback 4 are numbered **3000 and up**
(`llp-create --under 3000` for a child; extend LLP 3000 or LLP 2000.000
before minting a new number).

```
cargo build                          # the binary; no JS engine, no bootstrap; crates.io plus one vendored patch (vendor/tiny_http)
scripts/test.sh                      # everything blocking (60 s budget); reports every failure, never fail-fast
./target/debug/snapback4 --help      # init [--online|--expo] · check [--cost|--residency|--fix|--json] · dev · deploy · rollback · serve · run (an op, or a job as the system) · test · why · guide · data · sql · token (a service's seat) · doctor · device · types · --version
./target/debug/snapback4 guide       # lists cards (`guide aliases` lists aliases); `guide language` for the language card — every card's Quarry is compiled by the suite (crates/snapback4-lang/tests/guide.rs)
cd apps/chat && ../../target/debug/snapback4 check     # compiles snapback/*.q, writes snapback/generated/api.ts
cd apps/chat && ../../target/debug/snapback4 dev       # serves 127.0.0.1:4400; re-adopts the schema on save
cd apps/chat && bun run dev                            # the app on the real client (?as=bob picks a persona)
bun run --cwd packages/snapback4 typecheck   # the contract, the client and the hooks typecheck
cargo test -p snapback4-core --test corpus -- --nocapture   # the conformance corpus, with skip reasons
cargo test -p snapback4-difftest     # fixed seeds, oracle regressions and replay (opt-in, no database by default)
cargo run --release -p snapback4-difftest -- compile case.q   # a generated or hand-written source's binder diagnostics ("ok" when none)
cargo run --release -p snapback4-difftest -- run --seed 0 --cases 100000 --workers 6 --out /tmp/quarry-difftest
cargo run --release -p snapback4-difftest -- replay crates/snapback4-difftest/cases/found/rewrite-identity--why--NOT_FOUND
cargo run --release -p snapback4-difftest -- run-devices --seed 0 --cases 100000 --workers 8 --out /tmp/quarry-devices   # server against both device stores; no database
cargo run --release -p snapback4-difftest -- replay crates/snapback4-difftest/cases/found/device-sqlite--write-predicted-refused--admitted
(cd lean && lake build) && cargo run --release -p snapback4-difftest -- run-lean --seed 0 --cases 100000 --workers 8 --out /tmp/quarry-lean   # the engine against the Lean model (LLP 3000.035 §5.2); ~900 cases/s on 8 workers
cargo run --release -p snapback4-difftest -- replay 'crates/snapback4-difftest/cases/found/lean--kind--committed~NOT_FOUND'   # needs the model built, or SNAPBACK4_LEAN_ORACLE=<quarry-oracle>
./target/debug/snapback4 check --emit-lean schema.q   # one checked file as the Lean model's case JSON; programs outside its fragment listed with a reason
# Store tests require SNAPBACK4_EVAL_POSTGRES pointing to a private evaluation cluster.
cargo test -p snapback4-difftest --features postgres  # fails if the cluster is absent
cargo run --release -p snapback4-difftest --features postgres -- run-stores --seed 0 --cases 1000000 --workers 8 --seconds 10800 --out /tmp/quarry-stores
cargo run --release -p snapback4-difftest --features postgres -- replay /path/to/found-case
python3 scripts/test-difftest-scheduled.py  # runner/ticket tests; no schedule or push
scripts/difftest-scheduled.sh --checkout "$HOME/quarry-difftest-runner" --state-dir "$HOME/.snapback-quarry-difftest" --hours 3
# Operator only: install daily launchd (Darwin) or user systemd timer (Linux).
scripts/install-difftest-schedule.sh --checkout "$HOME/quarry-difftest-runner"
cargo test -p snapback4-sim          # the chat world under faults: the seven invariants
cargo test -p snapback4-sim --test exhaustive   # every sequence of two writes (two viewers, two groups): disclosure and write authority against a model of the rules
SNAPBACK4_EXHAUSTIVE_DEPTH=3 cargo test -p snapback4-sim --test exhaustive -- --ignored   # three writes: ~7,000 states, minutes
scripts/kani.sh [harness...]         # Kani proofs of the key encoding (crates/snapback4-core/src/key_proofs.rs; minutes, ~12 GB peak); outside test.sh
(cd lean && ./check.sh)              # the Lean model of Quarry's core (LLP 3000.035 §5.2): lake build (~15 s cold, needs elan), no-sorry/axiom audit, 53 examples, the quarry-oracle I/O fixture; outside test.sh; open points in lean/OPEN.md
cargo test -p snapback4-sim --test native   # native jobs: builds native-fixtures/rescore for wasm32-unknown-unknown (cold ~20 s, then cached under target/native-fixture) and runs it
bash scripts/build-wasm.sh           # the device as wasm into packages/snapback4/src/wasm/ (the web tier and the Chromium witness need it); fails when the gzip exceeds metrics/wasm-budget.json — a landing that needs the bytes raises the budget in the same commit, with the reason
node witness/idb.mjs                 # the Chromium witness: the client on the wasm device over IndexedDB (needs Chrome)
WITNESS_UDID=<dedicated-udid> node witness/expo.mjs   # published witness verb/helper; freshly install apps/chat-expo Release first (simctl uninstall a previous build: its store belongs to an earlier backend); port 4400, no Metro
node witness/scale.mjs [G] [N] [M]   # cold sync at scale: G groups × N messages × M members into the wasm device; wall time + JS heap → metrics/witness.jsonl
node scripts/measure-storage.mjs     # what the store weighs on the scale seed (rows, indexes, partition log) → metrics/storage.jsonl
                                     # SNAPBACK4_BIN=target/release/snapback4 measures the release build (throughput numbers)
node scripts/measure-client.mjs      # each client tier's bytes on the pinned toolchain → metrics/client.jsonl
node scripts/attribute-wasm.mjs [--families] [--check-families] [--check-absent] # class bytes and concrete serde families; --check-families refuses non-text readers/non-Vec writers outside the exact dynamic-envelope, counting-writer and Scan map-replay allowlists; content-buffer families name the duplicated types and refuse Deserialize representations using untagged, tag or flatten; --check-absent refuses the core's prefetch, admission or log-event symbols in the web artifact (needs twiggy; slow, outside test.sh)
bash scripts/check-release-guard.sh # the web artifact's configuration plus the debug-only `web-device-check` feature must refuse to build (in test.sh)
bash scripts/measure-wasm.sh         # the device core as wasm (crates/snapback4-device), family-checked, bound and measured → metrics/wasm.jsonl
bash scripts/build-xcframework.sh    # the device as an iOS xcframework for packages/snapback4-expo (staticlib alone, stripped; 13.2 MB with native reads)
node loopbar/run.mjs --n 3           # the loop bar: fresh agents on the published pin build reduced Clique M1–M3 → metrics/loopbar.jsonl
node scripts/publish.mjs --prepare-client # rebuild wasm + clean client, pack once for cross-host qualification
# Linux qualification host: strace >=6.8 on PATH (audits every successful TCP listen).
node scripts/qualify-linux.mjs --client /path/client.tgz --expo /path/expo.tgz --out /path/qualification # Linux suite + Ubuntu/Alpine proof, no publish
# Add --consumer-only for packed frontend/toolchain proofs without test.sh; this writes consumer.json, never a publication receipt.
node scripts/publish.mjs             # Mac: qualify/sign Darwin; optional --linux-receipt + --linux-tarball import; platforms before client
```

Crates: `snapback4-core` (value model, schema with `sync`, IR, key encoding,
SQLite store, rules with hoisting, the meter, the interpreter, the partition
log, at-most-once, the day-two classifier, wire forms), `snapback4-lang`
(Quarry lexer, parser, binder, diagnostics, the TypeScript emitter),
`snapback4` (verbs and the HTTP wire), `snapback4-sim` (the deterministic
world hosting the engine and a replica device), `snapback4-difftest` (seeded
Quarry source generator, rewrite and meter oracles, optional Postgres store
oracles with prefetch on/off, the server-against-device oracle on both device stores, the engine-against-Lean-model oracle, structural shrinking and replay; not published or linked by shipped crates), `snapback4-device` (the
interpreter over a `DeviceStore`: the core's SQLite under the native
module for Expo and Exact2 hosts, memory under wasm on the web; one
dispatcher, one suite on both)). The guide (`snapback4-lang/src/guide.rs`) is
the language card and the keyword cards; packages: `snapback4` (the client
over the device and the CLI shim), `snapback4-darwin-arm64` and `snapback4-linux-x64` (the binaries), `snapback4-expo`
(the Swift module over the device's C ABI).

What exists, by week (LLP 3000 §5):

| Week | Lands |
|---|---|
| 1 | the contract (`packages/snapback4/src/contract.ts`), the mocked chat app against it, the hub fixture wrapper, the five floor measurements in the journal, the simulator skeleton (`crates/snapback4-sim`), the Snapback 2 corpus copied in, 0.0.1 published |
| 2 | server: SQLite transaction and interpreter, rules and uniformity, the meter, the partition log (`sync`), generations, personas, the wire, `check`/`dev`/`run`/`test`/`why`/`guide`, the seven simulator invariants |
| 3 | web client: replica store, outbox with client-minted ids, local interpreter, reactive local queries, the online-only tier, the chat app on the real client |
| 4 | Expo client, day-two scenarios, deploy, the loop bar, the Clique hub run, the comparison record |

Gotchas you cannot derive from the code:

- Own Cargo and Bun workspaces; the repository root excludes them. Run cargo/bun from `snapback4/`. Node 24.x (`engines`): run the suite with the pinned runtime on PATH, not the ambient Homebrew node.
- Web builds require `bun install --frozen-lockfile` first. Binaryen 132.0.0 is
  an exact workspace devDependency; build/measurement invoke its portable
  `node_modules/binaryen/bin/wasm-opt` through Node, never a PATH optimizer.
  Missing/mismatched dependencies and optimizer failures refuse the build.
  See README's web-device build instructions and HV round 4 for host parity.
- The suite schedules client files in one pool: `max(1, min(24,
  floor(os.availableParallelism() / 3)))` slots (3 on 10 CPUs, 24 on 96).
  This counts Linux CPU affinity and leaves room for each file's servers,
  browser renderers and workers. Acquisition and server-e2e share that pool;
  there are no extra shard processes outside it. Real-timer files listed in
  `TIMER_CLIENT_FILES` drain that pool and run individually afterward with
  unchanged timing bounds. The quote-jump browser witness remains pooled.
  The two full-count model
  files retain a separate serial slot. `SNAPBACK4_TEST_SERIAL_CLIENT_FILES=1`
  still forces one client file at a time. The external file watchdog measures
  time since a subtest started or finished: max(900 seconds, largest single
  declared guard + max(60 seconds, 25%)). There is no absolute duration cap.
  Console output does not count as progress. Serial mode uses the same bounds.
  See `scripts/run-client-tests.mjs` and the QF round-2 guard census; update
  named work counts and recheck the census when declarations change. Every
  file runs as native `node --test` with default isolation, in a process group
  whose first member is a tiny Node keeper. The runner closes the keeper's stdin
  on completion, stall or abort; only the keeper calls `kill(0, SIGKILL)`.
  Runner death also closes that pipe. An unexpectedly exited keeper fails the
  file and never authorizes a runner signal. Cleanup requires two consecutive
  completed inspections (50 ms apart) with no living group member. Observed leftovers
  fail as TEST_FILE_LEAK. Failed listings and unclassifiable PID/PPID/PGID
  prefixes remain fail-closed. A ps timeout at two seconds retries once with a
  60-second bound; all other command errors refuse immediately. Listings use
  asynchronous spawn so other files' timers and keeper EOF delivery keep running.
  File watchdogs and cleanup's five-second window use per-file monotonic time.
  Expiry requires another completed snapshot: a live group after the deadline
  refuses; two consecutive empty snapshots pass even when the second is late.
  Precisely
  recognized exit-time routing anomalies
  discard the whole snapshot and retry at most twice, 50 ms apart; persistent
  anomalies refuse and never confer ownership (LLP 3000 §7, QF2).
  Metadata is validated for group members and
  their visible descendants; unrelated rows only increment diagnostics.
  Signed Darwin UIDs, variable lstart spacing and comm spaces are accepted.
  Vanished members count as exited. Processes leaving the group (setsid,
  setpgid or detached spawns) are outside cleanup custody; visible descendants
  receive metadata validation but are never signalled by the runner. Native Node's exit
  status alone decides test success; reporter counts are diagnostic only.
  The reporter observes progress as Node delivers it: synchronous work that
  does not yield may buffer events in Node's child until that work finishes.
  Such an unreported interval is subject to the same stall bound. See QF Round 7.
  The M5 MacBook is the publication host; the office M1 is calibration-only.
- Client tests and their helpers must gate Linux APIs (`/proc`, `strace`, `ss`,
  `taskset`, namespace/network tools) at the test or call, or implement a
  portable fallback. Keep portable assertions running on Darwin and give each
  skipped witness an explicit reason. The client pool runs
  `test-platform-audit.test.mjs`: a cheap static census follows static named/namespace `.mjs`
  imports and named helpers and checks scoped Linux skips/branches/early returns.
  A gate in another test does not count. This conservative common-case guard
  does not prove dynamic imports, generated scripts or arbitrary control flow;
  review those paths and run affected tests on the publication Mac.
  Listener snapshots and syscall tracing are Linux qualification capabilities,
  never dependencies of the published `snapback4/proof` browser helper.
- Before a publication dry run, warm the test artifacts **in that publication
  worktree**, while the host is free of calibration work:
  `LIBSQLITE3_FLAGS=-USQLITE_ENABLE_MEMORY_MANAGEMENT cargo test --workspace --no-run`.
  Use the dry run's toolchain, target directory, profile/features, `RUSTFLAGS`
  and Cargo profile environment. Do not add `--release` or `--profile production`:
  the suite uses the default test profile. The suite still asks Cargo for the
  complete test inventory, runs every binary and runs doctests separately.
  Warming compiles without executing tests and supplies no qualification receipt.
- One owner on the core (LLP 3000 decision 4): the engine, the store and the client contract are written by the lead session; Codex lanes take the fixture generator, the reference apps, the device witnesses and the periphery. Do not reshape `contract.ts` from a lane; propose the change in the journal.
- No ceiling is raised in slice one (LLP 2000.000 §12). A thing that does not fit changes the design or waits.
- The journal is append-only and dated; never rewrite an entry. LLP 3000 §7 carries decisions that must outlive it.
- Nothing here depends on `snapback2/` at runtime. Copy source deliberately, split under 1,500 lines on the way in, and say so in the journal.
- Named lane: `~/projects/snapback-sb4` on `sb4/*` branches. Never `git stash`; commit checkpoints.
- The conformance corpus (`corpus/`) is Snapback 2's serialized IR and value
  model; the core keeps those serializations so it binds. A case the slice
  cannot run is skipped with a named reason, never counted as a pass.
- `snapback/generated/api.ts` is built by `check` and `dev`, never committed;
  the app's typecheck needs a `check` first (the suite does one).
- Point rule probes and `exists` require a unique key. A fully pinned superset
  of a required unique key is unique too (for example an explicit role index
  containing the unique conversation/principal pair). Nullable unique key
  arguments need non-null proof. Other existence checks use bounded populations
  in code. Named equalities covering a total unique key check extra columns on
  that one row without an additional index.
- The packaged CLI uses `LIBSQLITE3_FLAGS=-USQLITE_ENABLE_MEMORY_MANAGEMENT` during both publication tests and its production build (LLP 3000 §7). A plain production Cargo build retains the dependency's ordinary SQLite policy; use the same build input when comparing with the package. The variable is unnecessary at runtime.
- When creating a stage, `scripts/publish.mjs` runs the suite and refuses on red, builds the production binary, packs `snapback4-darwin-arm64` and verifies the installed digest, then publishes both packages. Bump `version` in `packages/snapback4/package.json` (and its `optionalDependencies`), `packages/snapback4-darwin-arm64/package.json`,
  `packages/snapback4-linux-x64/package.json` and `Cargo.toml` together; a test refuses a mismatch.
- Publication retains exact qualified tarballs under `target/publication/`. If a
  stage exists, use `node scripts/publish.mjs --resume` to validate its recorded
  suite qualification, requalify those exact bytes through the installed-consumer
  proof, and finish missing registry artifacts; an existing version must match
  its staged integrity. This also applies after `--dry-run`. An ordinary invocation
  refuses an existing stage so changed source cannot silently publish old bytes.
  Bump the version to build a new pair; preserve a partially published stage.
- Cross-host publication (LLP 3000.021 §3.3): prepare the common client once
  from clean release source, qualify that tarball on Linux, and import the
  Linux tarball plus its receipt on the Mac. Source commit, client SRI and
  Linux SRI must agree. Linux prefers working Docker/Podman, otherwise uses checksum-pinned proot
  with clean Ubuntu 24.04/Alpine root filesystems in a user cache, and refuses
  `NPM_TOKEN` or `NODE_AUTH_TOKEN`, even when empty. It never signs or packs
  the client. The Mac never rebuilds or executes Linux; `--resume` rechecks
  its copied receipt. No receipt packs a Darwin-only client; an existing
  two-platform preparation is refused on that path. Packed dependencies must
  match the staged set. Uploads use verified private snapshots, with each
  digest checked again immediately before publish.
  Commands and current qualification limits:
  [`reports/20260927-lx-linux-package/REPORT.md`](./reports/20260927-lx-linux-package/REPORT.md).
- The guide is tested text. Every ```quarry block in `crates/snapback4-lang/src/guide.rs` and in `packages/snapback4/README.md` is compiled by `crates/snapback4-lang/tests/guide.rs` with no diagnostic and no note (the language card is also run against the engine); every refusal code the sources emit must be in `guide::CODES`; every `guide("..")` a diagnostic carries must name a card. Add a form to the language card when you add it to the parser, fence README Quarry as ```quarry, and never fence Quarry as plain text to dodge the test.
- Native jobs (LLP 3000.005) need `rustup target add wasm32-unknown-unknown`; the suite's fixture build fails loudly without it. The guest SDK `crates/snapback4-native` is a path dependency until it is published; an app's `snapback/native/Cargo.toml` names it by path.

Quarry store testing (LLP 3000.035 §5.1): `SNAPBACK4_EVAL_POSTGRES` is an
explicit evaluation connection. The factory owns one physical schema per worker,
restores its rows and bookkeeping, installs the compiled fixture, then opens the
ordinary engine. Mutation attempts restore a seeded snapshot; shrinking reuses
that same factory. `Database::connect_fresh()` is an opt-in test mode. Fresh and
restored outcomes must serialize to identical bytes. The local wire observer
retains SQLSTATE, message, detail and SQL statement before the public query API
converts a driver error to a refusal; it never changes the traffic.

Reads sweep every public boundary. Mutations compare every discovered position
twice on SQLite, and compare up to eight seeded positions per dimension on
Postgres (including first/last), plus the case's original budget. Sampling
counters are in `store_stats`; each selected position runs prefetch on and off.
Physical prefetch counters are separate from the exact public outcome record.
The widened generator (`src/widen.rs`) emits prefetch-reachable reads: pages
projecting `tags[.tag]` and `tags[name = .label]` (a `Get` and a unique
`Lookup` whose keys vary across rows) and a loop reading `tags[row.tag]`, so
`store_stats` reports marked reads, transferred rows, cases with a transfer
and `prefetch_consumption_witness_probes` (fewer database calls with prefetch
on: cached answers were consumed). A planted cached-hit-to-miss fault is caught
by the random corpus (LLP 3000.035 §5.1; the closed
[coverage ticket](../issues/closed/20261007-quarry-store-oracle-prefetch-coverage-gap.md)).
Heap is charged identically on every store and the device (LLP 3000.007
§20.24.5); any disagreement is a ticket candidate, never normalized into a pass.
The cluster regression asserts it finds no signature and refuses any new one.

`run-stores --seconds N` stops each worker between cases after N seconds; a case
and any shrinking already underway finish. `cases` remains the requested count,
`generated` is actual completed generation, and `time_limited_workers` reports
the early stop. Slowest-case timing includes generation, comparisons, shrinking
and artifact saving. It does not silently claim the requested million. The scheduled
runner rejects incomplete reports; its own `--hours` option instead chooses a
case count from a 256-case calibration of both campaigns, with 20% headroom and
a million-case cap. This is a sizing estimate, not a hard deadline.

Generator widening (`src/widen.rs`, LLP 3000.035 §5.1): from a stream
independent of the base generator (`generate::base`; a seed it leaves alone is
the base case byte for byte), cases gain prefetch-reachable reads, mutation
blocks (lets, `require`, `if`, several writes to `key` and `k2`, reads after
writes), a prelude of mutations `prep0..` run in order on the same store before
`run` (`Case::steps`, sometimes as the other viewer; `observe` then reads both
keys), and expiry (`expire at .until`, a clock advancing from `seeded_at`
through each step to `clock`, sweeps, `until: now + ttl`). Every oracle runs the
prelude (`execute::world`) and compares its outcomes (`prelude-*` fields;
`store-postgres-prelude`); a device acquires at the seed clock and catches up by
one sync round after the server's prelude. The prelude is emitted first so no
rewrite moves its sites. `lean::generate` narrows half the cases outside the
model (projections, expiry) back into its fragment; the rest stay named
unsupported (`shape`, `loop`, `table-expiry`). The report's `coverage` counts
each new feature (`block/*`, `prelude/*`, `projection/*`, `expire/*`, `clock/*`).

Quarry device testing (`src/devices.rs`, `src/device_writes.rs`, LLP 3000.035
§5.1 oracle 4): each case runs on a server engine and on a device of each store
(native SQLite at a file path, and memory), both brought up through the client's
own path: the `/schema` backend as JSON text, then `local.ts::syncRound` (capture
`sync_state`, `/sync` snapshot pages built as `serve_sync.rs` builds them,
`observe_store`, `apply` through `call_text`, catch-up stream). Never seed a
device store directly. Reads the device publishes without the server (`complete`
with `unknown` false, or a known refusal) compare the whole outcome record
except `seq` (and server-only `delivery`); every other read counts under a named
reason (`online-only-*`). Low ceilings reach the device only through `call`'s
`spent` ledger (`default - n`), compared at the server's boundary positions.
Writes go through `admit`: a device refusal must equal the server's, a
predicted row must equal the committed one (less its `pending` marker), and
after `settle` plus a sync round the device must answer `observe` as the server
does. `run-devices` accepts `--seconds`. Known findings are retained under
`cases/found/device-*` with a `DIAGNOSIS.md`; `tests/devices.rs` replays them
and `tests/smoke.rs` pins the fixed-seed signature set.

The Lean oracle (`run-lean`, LLP 3000.035 §5.2): `snapback4_lang::emit::lean`
translates each generated program's surface tree (never the binder's IR) to
the model's case JSON; `quarry-oracle` (`lean/Oracle.lean`, one process per
worker) answers one tagged outcome per line; the engine runs on SQLite at
default ceilings. Compared: kind, refusal code, data (tagged: an id is not a
text), `complete`, `next` presence, each side's own continuation, and a
mutation's `observe` reread. Not compared: meter fields and sites. Programs
outside the model's fragment are named unsupported reasons, never passes.
`lean::generate` adds Lean-only variants (per-effect rules, a hidden probe
target, nullable/id orders, residual filters) aimed at `lean/OPEN.md`. A
disagreement is a Rust defect, a model defect (only when a document
contradicts the model: fix the model, rerun `lean/check.sh`), or an open
point (record the engine's behaviour in `OPEN.md`); never change the model to
match the engine without a document. Findings live in
`cases/found/lean--*` with `DIAGNOSIS.md`; `tests/lean.rs` replays them and
pins the open signature set. Lean-dependent tests skip loudly without the
built model; `SNAPBACK4_REQUIRE_LEAN=1` makes that a failure.

Quarry scheduled testing: prepare a separate clean clone whose current branch
can fast-forward to `origin/main`. The runner fetches/fast-forwards it and starts
its own socket-only evaluation cluster with `fsync=off`,
`synchronous_commit=off`, `full_page_writes=off`, `shared_buffers=1GB`, and
`max_connections=200`. It builds the Lean model's `quarry-oracle` first
(`lake build` in `lean/`; a host without `lake` fails the run), then runs the
SQLite, store, device and Lean campaigns on the selected
seed window, replays shrunk findings twice, and files/appends open `issues/*.md`
tickets. Only ticket changes are committed/pushed, with three fetch/rebase
retries. State and reports live outside Git under `--state-dir`; seeds advance
only after completed work and successful owned-cluster cleanup. Dirty/diverged
trees, overlap, setup failures, partial reports and failed publication refuse.
A crash can retain the lock and cluster directory; inspect `lock/owner.json`
and the run report. Never stop a cluster by process name.

`--dry-run --cases 32` writes previews and a separate `next-seed.dry-run.json`,
never commits or pushes. Dry runs may explicitly supply `--external-postgres
'host=... user=... dbname=...'`; that cluster is neither started nor stopped by
the runner. Ambient connections do not override normal private-cluster ownership.
The installer remains an operator action; do not invoke it during development.

The Lean oracle on a scheduled host (one-time, per user, no sudo): install
elan with `curl https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leanprover/elan/master/elan-init.sh -sSf | sh -s -- -y --default-toolchain none`.
The runner finds `lake` on `PATH` or in `~/.elan/bin`; the first `lake build`
downloads the toolchain pinned in `lean/lean-toolchain` (Lean 4.34.1, no
Mathlib), so the first scheduled run needs network. Check by hand with
`(cd lean && ./check.sh)` in the runner's clone. `run.json` records the pinned
toolchain and the oracle binary's digest.

## 13. Wave 15 authorization recipes (RULES5)

This amendment specifies applications of existing authorization semantics on
LIVE, rather than extending the language or relaxing a rule. It changes no
planner, protocol, adoption, ids, expiry, client contract or engine ceilings.
The commission is wave-15 fix list #4 excluding its two LANG4 note bullets.

### 13.1 Refusal ladder and probes

The rules card executes three standalone schemas: an asserted hidden lookup
refuses NOT_FOUND before a later state requirement; a hidden secondary unique
conflict suppresses even an authored `else` code and returns E_RULE; and a
rule `exists` can admit a write using a row that a caller-filtered program
`exists` cannot read. Statement order and rollback still govern, not a new
universal phase order. Successful blind writes can intentionally disclose that
their rule held. No hidden conflict key, clause or row enters the public refusal.
The exact program-probe teaching block intentionally emits one
N_UNREADABLE_PROBE; compile and packed tests pin its code and site. It teaches
caller-visible existence, never global absence enforcement.

### 13.2 Recipe invariants

- Readable totals pin the caller's owner scope. They return the complete domain
  total or unavailable; they never expose a subtotal as a complete total.
- Durable handoffs use server `connected` in the mutation and independently
  enforce enrolled-recipient/sender rules. Poll completion retains the existing
  35-second grace. Acceptance during that window is intentional and does not
  assert receipt or future connectivity. Offline calls queue without prediction.
  Ephemeral tables cannot grant durable authority (LLP 3000.017).
- Scheduled publication stores private current draft revision plus approval;
  edits and withdrawal retract the public copy atomically. Editing clears
  approval. Reapproval retracts any previous copy before choosing a new due
  time. The system's stable-id sweep publishes only approved due revisions;
  system jobs bypass source rules, so approval is checked in that job. Ordinary
  callers have no public-copy insert/update rule. Coalescing publishes at the
  next due run, with no exact-instant or downloaded-byte recall claim.
- Private bookings expose a reviewed maintained count, including empty-group
  zero, rather than a caller-filtered capacity count. Admission and insert are
  one writer transaction. All future booking writers must share the check;
  immutable capacity prevents silent shrinking below occupancy. Public count
  deltas are an intentional disclosure, not protection from timing inference.
- The leaderboard keeps attempts private and publishes exact totals only for
  opted-in owners. Every attempt writer refreshes an existing entry atomically;
  opting out deletes it. Names, principals, totals, deltas and relative rank
  become public by consent. Projection alone does not hide source columns.
- Versioned transitions first read the private task, then check expected version
  and the permitted status edge, update and append private history atomically.
  A named old/next rule independently enforces the status edge. Version/history
  invariants belong to every future writer; a failed history insert rolls back
  the update. There is no new generic compare-and-swap or automatic history.

### 13.3 Three levels and LIVE admission

LLP 3000.014.000 remains the authority: complete current-parent rule, immutable
required direct reference, exact product cap and bounded atomic maintenance.
The three-level recipe flattens the authority root onto each level, so the
folder and file are separate direct live children of the ordinary project.
The file's immutable project reference is checked against its folder at insert.
It is a hierarchy routing copy, not mutable copied authority. Project transfer
updates both live-child families in one transaction. This is valid only when
all levels share exactly that root's readers.

A chain of live children remains refused. Wave-15 lane 077 has additional
section-owner grants, so a proposal-only inherited rule would widen access.
Its stable copied ancestor ids plus exact online probes remain appropriate
for those distinct policies. Captured read-through cannot track mutable state.
However, 077's checks can directly inherit their ordinary section's whole
custom rule, whose probe reads the proposal's current state, without making
that section a live child. Existing routing keys used by writers/queries remain
validated. This online policy preserves the section-owner branch.
Lane 085's entries can inherit the complete service rule online once its
writing cascade action is removed and capped explicit child deletion replaces
it. Its unpinned cross-service hymnUsage total remains unproved after the live
conversion; preserve its explicit probe rule. Adding a maintained view over
the live endpoint also violates the first form's derivation boundary. With
cascade retained the live composition stays refused; no reader branch
or reference action may silently disappear. Mutable audience copies remain the
atomic application-maintained choice for unsupported live compositions or a
different intended child audience; immutable copies preserve creation access.

### 13.4 Qualification and review

Every added Quarry block is extracted from the installed packed CLI and runs
through the packed client/native packages in the existing publication-rules
qualification path. Tests cover positive, hidden and refused cases, totals,
capacity, consent withdrawal, grace/reconnect, scheduled withdrawal/edit/
reapproval, ancestor transfer and stale/private history. Language guide checks
compile all blocks and allow only the one explicitly taught probe note.
The 077/085 original schemas and live candidates are surveyed read-only and
the precise supported/refused shapes are retained under reports/wave15-rules5.
Paired independent Muse and fresh Codex reviews and disposition are recorded
below when received. LANG4 owns query-audience and N_REQUIRE_REFUSED notes.

 succeeded in 0ms:
# Rules

**Start here:** Reactions inherit their message's conversation membership; this excerpt uses the complete schema below. The dotted rule and audience are sugar for `read through` and inferred `sync`, including parent lifetime and insert authority.

```quarry
table reactions:
  message: messages
  immutable message
  read <- exists members[.message.conversation, viewer]
  sync to members[.message.conversation] last 500 by .id
```

- `E_READ_THROUGH`: the path must spell the whole parent rule; use a required immutable reference without a default, and immutable parent rule/expiry fields.
- `E_QUARRY_SELF_EQUALITY`: a nested predicate compares its row to itself; capture the outer value in a local before entering that predicate.
- `E_RULE`: a write lacks authority (mutations write as the caller; omitted rules deny); require a live readable parent and the child's insert rule, and under `dev` read the refusing clause with `snapback4 why recent`.

## Reference

### Hops, operators and read through
Write rules and `after insert` keys allow three live hops: `.reaction.message.conversation`, including `exists members[.message.conversation, viewer]`. `messages[id = .message].conversation` is equivalent. Every target must be live and readable; absent, expired or hidden targets remain unknown under `not` and `= null`. Program lookups (`t[id] !`, `exists`) are reads too: a row the caller cannot read is NOT_FOUND there, before any later `require` or the delete/update rule runs, so give a moderator read access to what they moderate, or delete by id without a lookup and let the delete rule decide (`guide refusals` has the recipe). `check` notes N_UNREADABLE_PROBE on guards without proved read authority; use unique/cap/after insert constraints for global absence across hidden rows.
Each occurrence prepays one probe, including skipped branches. `check --cost`/`why` list them; prediction needs every read covered.
Insert uses the new image; an unnamed `update <- R` must hold for both the row before and the row after the write (pair `update <- .owner = viewer` with `immutable owner`); a named `update (old, next)` judges exactly what it names; delete uses its pre-delete image; final obligations resolve at transaction end. Read-through capture/lifetimes stay unchanged. Null guards narrow only the same term and image.
Beyond three hops, private/maintained fields and ephemeral targets refuse E_QUARRY_RULE_HOP with concrete mutation `require` checks. A rule says `=`/`!=` over `.col`, `viewer` and literals, `exists`/`created` probes and `and`/`or`/`not`; it orders (`<`, `>=`) only an insert row's own required int/time/decimal/money columns (`.stars >= 1`; an integer literal beside money is minor units) and `update (old, next)` images of the same types. The clock, arithmetic, helper calls and `else CODE` are E_QUARRY_RULE_OPERATOR: a `require … else CODE` in the mutation (`snapback4 why E_QUARRY_RULE_OPERATOR` prints the table of rule forms).

```quarry
table orgs:
  read <- exists members[.id, viewer]
  insert <- allow
  online only
table members:
  org: orgs
  principal: principal
  unique org, principal
  read <- .principal = viewer
  insert <- created orgs[.org] or exists members[.org, viewer]
  online only
table issues:
  org: orgs
  title: text <= 200
  comments: count comments by issue
  immutable org
  read through .org
  insert <- allow
  online only
table comments:
  issue: issues
  author: principal
  body: text <= 2000
  immutable issue
  read through .issue
  insert <- .author = viewer
  delete <- .author = viewer
  online only
query discussion(issue: issues, c: cursor ?):
  comments where .issue = issue first 20 by .id after c
```

### A child inherits its parent's readers
A child names one required immutable reference, without a default, to an ordinary application table. `read through` is its whole read rule; put service grants on the parent. The parent's read-rule fields and authored expiry must be immutable. An insert also needs a live parent readable by the writer, before the child's insert rule runs. The engine captures those inputs; they travel with sync images but never appear in query rows or accept payload values.

Each child expires no later than its parent, through any number of levels. A parent's delete needs read authority and waits until its children are deleted and expired children swept. Its id cannot be reused while physical children remain. A mutable child deadline can shorten its life but cannot extend it past the parent.

`sync` infers exactly the parent's substituted audience after service grants are removed. A conjunction such as membership and an archived flag stays `online only`; `check --verbose` explains its server reads. A parent-pinned page resolves authorization once, while completeness still requires the child's own coverage. An unknown or mismatched parent takes the server fallback. Retained history stays marked `retained: true` beside the server's current page. On these syncing delivered-history children, `retained` is row metadata; name an application column `wasRetained` instead. Row shapes and spreads preserve the mark.

A device predicts a plain child insert only with a live parent covered by its current partition, or one predicted in the same pending chain. An unheld, expired or retained-only parent queues without a row. Upserts and `insert … if absent` need complete child-group coverage to prove absence; changed unique keys or cap groups need their populations covered. A parent delete requires full current child coverage and no live children. Expiry uses the later of the device's supplied clock and the last atomically applied server time.

### A child follows a mutable parent audience

`read through live .parent` maintains the whole current parent rule. Require `immutable parent` without a default and `cap N by parent`. Parent writes prepay maximum fanout, update children atomically and queue on devices. Bare `read through` stays captured.

Use ordinary nonexpiring endpoints/probes and bounded plain indexes. Chains, derived/private/receipt endpoints and writing reference actions refuse. Sync requires one exact all-current/until-revoked audience; other whole rules work online.

`N_LIVE_PARENT_COST` means fixed fanout exceeds ordinary ceilings. Reduce caps or row/index bounds, or maintain manually. Parent writes queue on devices.

Parent/roster changes invalidate; matching copies and child coverage prove authority. Adoption validates copies; restoration uses current parents. Rebuild for `live-parent-copy-v1`. Bulk calls share one job account. See `guide rule-recipes current-roster`.

### Clique receipts and reactions
Clique receipts and reactions need only their message reference. The unread view uses the captured conversation field and inherits its rule, without an explicit `read` or copied deadline. These parents stay online, so reaction pages use the server:

```quarry
table conversations:
  read <- exists members[.id, viewer]
  insert <- allow
  online only

table members:
  conversation: conversations
  principal: principal
  immutable conversation, principal
  unique conversation, principal
  read <- exists members[.conversation, viewer]
  insert <- created conversations[.conversation] or exists members[.conversation, viewer]
  online only

table messages:
  conversation: conversations
  sender: principal
  body: text <= 2000
  until: time ?
  immutable conversation, until
  read <- exists members[.conversation, viewer]
  insert <- .sender = viewer and exists members[.conversation, viewer]
  expire at .until
  online only

table receipts:
  message: messages
  principal: principal
  state: int
  immutable message, principal
  unique message, principal
  read through .message
  insert <- created messages[.message] or .principal = viewer
  update <- .principal = viewer
  online only

table reactions:
  message: messages
  immutable message
  read <- exists members[.message.conversation, viewer]
  sync to members[.message.conversation] last 500 by .id
  principal: principal
  emoji: text <= 20
  immutable principal
  unique message, principal
  insert <- .principal = viewer and exists members[.message.conversation, viewer]
  delete <- .principal = viewer and exists members[.message.conversation, viewer]

view unread: count receipts where .state < 2 by .message.conversation, .principal
  online only

query unreadFor(conversation: conversations):
  unread[conversation, viewer]
query received(message: messages, c: cursor ?):
  receipts where .message = message first 50 by .principal after c { id, principal, state }
query reacted(message: messages, c: cursor ?):
  reactions where .message = message first 50 by .principal after c { id, principal, emoji }
```

### Row rules, probes and rule spelling

A bare `exists` probe in a table rule never inherits the target's read rule: it tests the keyed row even when the caller cannot read it. Program `exists` is a caller-filtered read. Dotted membership hops in read rules are only `read through` (whole parent rule, immutable captures and inherited lifetime). An online-only one-hop equality may instead lower to its explicit keyed probe, as described below; it also inherits no target read rule. For the parent's current department roster, use `guide rule-recipes current-roster`.

A view grouped directly by the parent reference is read by key, such as `totals[issue]`; scanning its groups is refused. An unknown parent or unknown child coverage leaves that device count incomplete, never an invented zero. For independent row rules, use the comparisons and keyed probes below.

```quarry
table docs:
  ownerId: principal
  title: text
  state: enum('draft', 'final')
  read <- .ownerId = viewer or exists editors[.id, viewer]                  -- allow | deny | public ['why'] | .col = viewer | .col != 'x' | exists t[..] | and | or | not
  insert <- .ownerId = viewer and .state = 'draft'
  update (old, next) <- old.ownerId = viewer and next.ownerId = viewer and old.state != 'final'   -- both images of an update
  delete <- .ownerId = viewer

table editors:
  docId: docs
  principal: principal
  unique docId, principal
  read <- exists editors[.docId, viewer]
  insert <- exists docs[.docId, ownerId = viewer] or created docs[.docId]   -- a probe may name its columns; created: this mutation inserted the row
  delete <- .principal = viewer
```

Each of `read`, `insert`, `update` and `delete` takes one rule over the row (`.col`), `viewer` and literals. A table without a rule for an effect denies it. `public 'why'` on its own line is the read rule. The compiler supplies indexes for `exists` probes; named equalities may cover a total unique key and check extra columns on that one row, for both `exists` and `created`, without an extra index. A full positional unique key may come first, followed by named extra-column tests, as in `docs[.docId, ownerId = viewer]`. Every focal field used by the key or extra tests must be pinned for one verdict to serve a whole page. `created t[..]` is valid in insert rules only. A rule cannot vary with time (use `expire`). A read rule that is exactly `exists t[.a, .b, viewer]`, `.col = viewer` or `public` can be synced (guide sync). The system principal consults no rules, so `system` is never a rule word. An audience read rule may add `or viewer = 'service:pusher'` (or several service grants); sync infers its audience without them, and ordinary extra disjuncts still refuse.

E_QUARRY_RULE_SPELLING locates a guessed table rule: `select <-` and `view <-` become `read <-`, `create <-` becomes `insert <-`, `modify <-` and `edit <-` become `update <-`, and `remove <-` becomes `delete <-`. For `write <-`, declare separate insert, update and delete rules for the operations intended.

### What a refused write returns: E_RULE, NOT_FOUND, E_UNIQUE, E_CONSTRAINT

A mutation's `require` decides intent; each write still passes its table's rule. An unnamed update rule judges old and next. For a deliberate pre-write gate, use `update (old, next) <- old.owner = viewer and old.state = 'pending'`; that permits leaving pending, so constrain next too when the policy needs it. `E_RULE` identifies table authorization; `why recent` privately names the failing old/next image (or the named transition). Hidden targets retain `NOT_FOUND`, with no image or clause exposed to the caller. See: `guide rule-recipes update-images`.

For update/upsert, "readable" means the read rule admits the old row in the pre-write state, before the patch or any of its effects. The engine resolves id and unique-key targets privately; the update rule judges old and next.

A mutation's writes pass the table's insert, update and delete rules as the caller; only a job runs as the system. `check` also notes N_WRITE_DENIED (table authorization layer and contradictory old/next image) when preceding caller conditions provably contradict the update rule, including the update arm of an upsert; N_LOOKUP_NEVER_VISIBLE identifies a nullable point lookup whose read rule contradicts those conditions. Unknown role overlap is quiet. `check` notes N_WRITE_DENIED for a mutation's write (or upsert arm) whose rule is `deny` or omitted: write the rule its callers need, or do the write in a job. It notes the same code for a counter (`stock: v.stock - 1`) on a table whose update rule admits only a role, in a mutation that ordinary callers use and that never requires the role: keep the count as a maintained view over the rows they write instead. `check` notes N_READ_DENIED for a query or mutation that looks up an aggregate by key whose read rule is `deny`: every caller reads `null` for both a stored and absent group, so give it the rule its readers need over its group keys. In development, `snapback4 run` prints the clause that refused a write and its line; the refusal a client sees never names it. `insert` and `insert ... if absent` return E_RULE when the insert rule denies the viewer. A unique clash with a hidden row returns E_RULE for either insert form. For a readable secondary unique clash without a custom refusal, ordinary insert returns E_UNIQUE naming the declared key, while `insert ... if absent` succeeds with null. If the update rule denies an update or upsert-patch, it returns E_RULE for a readable target and NOT_FOUND for an unreadable target. A valid update or upsert-patch admitted by the update rule can change a hidden row; if the resulting row remains hidden, it returns null. Without a custom unique refusal, an update or upsert-patch with a secondary unique clash returns NOT_FOUND if its target is unreadable, E_RULE if only the colliding row is unreadable, and E_UNIQUE naming the declared key if both are readable. An update condition on an unreadable target returns NOT_FOUND even if it would match; on a readable target, a false condition returns E_CONFLICT with a read-rule `current`. Delete returns NOT_FOUND for a missing id or an unreadable refused target, and E_RULE for a readable row whose delete rule refuses. Reading a hidden row returns null. Use a write admitted by its rule; NOT_FOUND on an update or upsert-patch does not prove absence.

| Update/upsert target before the write | Refusal details |
| --- | --- |
| Readable | `E_RULE`; `E_CONFLICT` with a read-rule `current`; named `E_UNIQUE` for declared keys; `E_CONSTRAINT` for other constraints; `E_INPUT` |
| Unreadable or absent | `NOT_FOUND` for rule denial, an `update` condition, secondary unique clash, or an incomplete insert patch; no row, index, column or branch detail |

Use `upsert counters[recipient] { count: 1 }` under an `update` rule to write another principal's row. Success discloses the admitted row's existence and the update rule predicates. It grants no extra stream or snapshot row. A condition belongs on `update`; an upsert inserts or patches. A condition needs a row you can read; a blind patch needs none. A blind update or upsert-patch needs no read rule: the update rule judges the old and next images; a preceding program lookup still needs read access. On a row you cannot read, immutable columns in a patch are ignored. A readable immutable mismatch still names the column. Before target lookup, the engine reserves room for the rule probes and the largest merged image allowed by the schema and supplied patch; if it cannot fit, `E_BOUND` refuses equally for a hidden or absent target. A readable aggregate view over a hidden source is a declared read channel: its delta can disclose the old contribution. `N_MAINTAIN_DELTA` notes potentially wider update authority under a separately readable aggregate view. Restrict its read audience or source write authority if that delta is private.

### Refusal ladder

Run the statements from top to bottom. The first refusal wins, and the whole mutation rolls back. An asserted program read checks visibility before a later business `require`. Here bob gets `NOT_FOUND` for alice's row even if its state is already closed; alice gets `CLOSED`. Moving a role-only guard before the lookup gives that guard priority without revealing a hidden row.

```quarry
use personas alice, bob
table ladderTickets:
  owner: principal
  state: enum('open', 'closed')
  immutable owner
  read <- .owner = viewer
  insert <- .owner = viewer
  update <- .owner = viewer
  online only
mutation closeLadderTicket(ticket: ladderTickets):
  t = ladderTickets[ticket] ! else NOT_FOUND
  require t.state = 'open' else CLOSED
  update ladderTickets[ticket] { state: 'closed' }
```

A uniqueness constraint sees physical conflicts that program reads cannot. For this recipe alice inserting her own row with bob's hidden key gets `E_RULE`, not `KEY_TAKEN`; her own readable conflict gets `KEY_TAKEN`. The hidden refusal exposes neither the key name nor the conflicting row. Use a declared constraint for global uniqueness; a program `not exists` only proves caller-visible absence.

```quarry
use personas alice, bob
table ladderKeys:
  owner: principal
  key: text 1..40
  unique key else KEY_TAKEN
  immutable owner, key
  read <- .owner = viewer
  insert <- .owner = viewer
  online only
mutation claimLadderKey(key: text 1..40):
  return insert ladderKeys { owner: viewer, key }
```

Rule `exists` and program `exists`, side by side: a rule probe evaluates the keyed target without borrowing its read rule. A program probe filters it by the caller's read authority. In this example bob's hidden grant lets alice write a note, while alice's `canReadLadderGrant(bob)` is false. A rule probe does not return the grant or make it program-readable. This deliberately allows the note writer to discover whether the grant permits that write. `check` deliberately prints `N_UNREADABLE_PROBE` for canReadLadderGrant: caller-visible existence is intended here; never use its false result to enforce global absence. The qualification test pins that one note.

```quarry
use personas alice, bob
table ladderGrants:
  principal: principal
  unique principal
  read <- .principal = viewer
  online only
table ladderNotes:
  grant: principal
  author: principal
  immutable grant, author
  read <- .author = viewer
  insert <- .author = viewer and exists ladderGrants[.grant]
  online only
mutation writeLadderNote(grant: principal):
  return insert ladderNotes { grant, author: viewer }
query canReadLadderGrant(grant: principal):
  return exists ladderGrants[grant]
```

For a body written as asserted lookup → write → require, the ladder is: decode (`E_INPUT`) → hidden lookup (`NOT_FOUND`) → table rule (`E_RULE` on a readable denied target) → the authored `require` code. After decoding, actual statement order decides: a require before a write runs first; a later require cannot replace an earlier refusal. A nullable hidden lookup returns null; an asserted lookup uses its authored `else` code, conventionally NOT_FOUND. Hidden update/delete denial stays NOT_FOUND. Mutations roll back earlier writes.

An upsert follows the same statement-order ladder. On a readable existing row whose update rule refuses, ladderPut returns E_RULE; on a hidden denied row it returns NOT_FOUND. An absent own row passes insert, then AFTER_WRITE refuses and rolls that insert back. The later require cannot rename the write's refusal.

```quarry
use personas alice, bob, carol
table ladderCounters:
  owner: principal
  value: int
  open: bool default false
  unique owner
  immutable owner
  read <- .owner = viewer
  insert <- .owner = viewer
  update <- .owner = viewer and .open = true
  online only
mutation ladderPut(owner: principal):
  upsert ladderCounters[owner = owner] { owner, value: 1 }
  require false else AFTER_WRITE
query ladderCounter(owner: principal):
  return ladderCounters[owner = owner]
```

A query over hidden rows is complete and empty when its scope uniformly refuses the viewer. A nonuniform scan may be partial even with zero visible rows; an empty list alone proves neither access nor physical absence. See: `guide rule-recipes role-workflow` for independently enforced table and mutation authority, and `guide refusals` for write-internal ordering.

### Disclosure: aggregates, assets and maintained columns
Omitted asset columns keep their placement. An explicit asset id on a row you
cannot read must be your own upload, even if it equals the retained id. A rule
probe costs one unit regardless of expired siblings; a uniform false page is
decided from its pinned prefix and returns empty and complete at no charge.

A filtered count can disclose whether a hidden row matched its predicate; computed groups disclose bucket membership and rank discloses relative values. The view's filter grants no source-row read. N_MAINTAIN_DELTA names the filter/key/value dependencies and the separately readable target, including insert/delete channels. Restrict its read audience to close the channel:

```quarry
table posts:
  authorId: principal
  parentId: id ?
  at: time
  read <- .authorId = viewer
  insert <- .authorId = viewer
  update <- allow
  online only
view originalPostCounts: count posts where .parentId = null by .authorId
  read <- .authorId = viewer
  online only
query ownOriginals():
  return originalPostCounts[viewer]
```

Here a writer may update a hidden post, but cannot read that author's count. Changing the aggregate view to read <- allow deliberately grants the filtered-delta channel and produces N_MAINTAIN_DELTA. Hidden-target attempts reserve both possible aggregate groups, predicate/key work and indexes before inspecting the old source image.

A maintained column such as `likeCount: count likes by postId` belongs to the engine, while ordinary fields keep their existing writer rules. Source rules authorize contributions; parent readers receive the count, including counts over private sources. Rules and their probes cannot depend on maintained fields. Adding or redefining the column recounts and mirrors live parents without consulting parent write rules; its event-free generation barrier reacquires devices and wakes server-run parent watches. See `guide maintains`.

## Field authority: written by

`readUpTo: int default 0 written by .principal` gives that field its own writer: the required `principal` column on the same row. An insert by anyone else omits it and receives the declared default. Supplying even the default explicitly requires ownership. On update and the patch arm of upsert, the old row's owner authorizes each touched owned field. Other touched fields still require the row's update rule. An owner can patch just their fields when the table has no update rule. A named `update (old, next)` rule also constrains owner writes; it can compare matching integers or times with `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=`. Read rules keep their equality-only contract.

### Private owned fields
The field is private to its owner unless declared `readable by audience`.
Group/public log images omit private fields; the owner's principal partition
carries their image, and every owner's device receives it while the row is
readable. Queries, shapes, write results and snapshots apply the same
projection. Selecting an omitted field returns null, including in `{ * }` and as a `group`
or `sort` key; generated row types
mark the field optional. Asset downloads apply the same field visibility.
Field ownership does not grant read access to the row. Ownership changes
redact the former owner's copy, or remove it if the row also becomes unreadable.
A device queues a transition that needs another owner's omitted value or
would acquire ownership of that value. An independent owned-field patch on a
held row predicts when its own transition and resource limits permit it.
Untouched self-equalities do not require the omitted value. Ordinary row grants do not add dependencies to such a patch.
Unknown owner-delivery witnesses also do not prevent that prediction; the
server decides which owners may receive the committed image.
Private-field rule inputs require a named transition and ownership or audience
visibility; ordinary writers cannot test another owner's private value.
Accounting, nullable self-comparisons and expiring authority witnesses are
specified in `snapback4 why E_FIELD_AUTHORITY`.

Clique read marks use this recipe. Add `readable by audience` after `written by .principal` when other conversation members should see receipts. The monotonic rule is an application decision, enforced on the server and under prediction. `markRead` predicts on a held row, including offline.

```quarry
table conversations:
  creator: principal
  read <- exists members[.id, viewer]
  insert <- .creator = viewer
  sync to members[.id]
table members:
  conversationId: conversations
  principal: principal
  readUpTo: int default 0 written by .principal
  unique conversationId, principal
  cap 100 by conversationId
  immutable conversationId, principal
  read <- exists members[.conversationId, viewer]
  insert <- created conversations[.conversationId] or exists members[.conversationId, viewer]
  update (old, next) <- next.readUpTo >= old.readUpTo
  sync to members[.conversationId]
mutation markRead(memberId: members, readUpTo: int) predicts:
  update members[memberId] { readUpTo }
```

E_FIELD_AUTHORITY means an owner declaration is invalid, or a supplied/updated field is not yours. Name a required principal column on this table, declare a default, and omit the field when writing another principal's row. A private field in an index or aggregate needs `readable by audience` (after `written by`) when that disclosure fits. An unreadable update or refused delete target, and an absent upsert refused by insert authority or field ownership, keep `NOT_FOUND` with the same refusal and meter. Direct inserts and readable targets keep `E_FIELD_AUTHORITY`; the system keeps its rule bypass. A page over private fields must "scope this read to a uniformly readable audience" (a walk over hidden rows counts them): `check` refuses a scan its pins do not decide, role guard or not. Pin the read rule's column (`visits where .parent = viewer`), move the field to its own table keyed by the row, or make it `readable by audience`.

Read-through repairs: `why E_READ_THROUGH`, `why E_READ_THROUGH_ADOPT`, `why E_PARENT_HAS_CHILDREN` and `why E_READ_THROUGH_SCAN`.

Membership paths spelling the whole parent rule lower to `read through`. Without sync, a one-hop read-rule equality (`.author = viewer or .goal.owner = viewer`) is the probe `exists goals[.goal, owner = viewer]`; other read reference expressions (two hops, `!=`, a synced table) remain unsupported. Write rules admit live chains as above. Parent-key aggregates inherit the read rule. A diagnostic never suggests dropping the parent hop. An aggregate read line proved identical to the parent's rule has a whole-line repair; additional conditions remain a modeling choice and are not removed by `--fix`.

exec
/bin/bash -lc 'timeout 10s cat snapback4/crates/snapback4-lang/src/guide/rule_recipes_wave15.md' in /home/ccheever/projects/sb4-w15-rules5
 succeeded in 0ms:

## Totals over the caller's readable rows

`guide rule-recipes readable-totals` pins the owner that decides the whole
source rule. Hidden rows never enter this caller's count or sum. A shared
total is a separate disclosure decision (`guide totals`); do not remove the
owner pin to make a capacity check over private rows.

```quarry
use personas alice, bob, carol
table personalCosts:
  owner: principal
  cents: int 0..1000000
  immutable owner
  read <- .owner = viewer
  insert <- .owner = viewer
  online only
mutation addPersonalCost(cents: int 0..1000000):
  return insert personalCosts { owner: viewer, cents }
query readableTotals():
  return { n: count(personalCosts where .owner = viewer), cents: sum(personalCosts where .owner = viewer, .cents) }
```

These online totals cover the complete authorized domain or return unavailable
at a meter cut. They never return a partial number. For an arbitrary mixture
of partly readable rows, page the rows and label a sum of that page as a page
subtotal, or materialize a separately authorized view.

## Presence-gated durable writes with disconnect grace

`guide rule-recipes presence-write` requires an enrolled peer and the server's
`connected` verdict before recording a durable handoff. Presence belongs in
the mutation, since table rules cannot read it. Durable rules independently
restrict the writer and recipient. This is a server decision: the mutation
queues offline and predicts no handoff. Ephemeral room indicators cannot
authorize durable writes (`guide ephemeral`).

```quarry
use personas alice, bob, carol
table handoffPeers:
  principal: principal
  unique principal
  read <- .principal = viewer or viewer = 'service:ops'
  insert <- viewer = 'service:ops'
  online only
table durableHandoffs:
  sender: principal
  recipient: principal
  body: text 1..200
  immutable sender, recipient, body
  read <- .sender = viewer or .recipient = viewer
  insert <- .sender = viewer and exists handoffPeers[.recipient]
  online only
mutation enrollHandoffPeer(principal: principal):
  require viewer = 'service:ops' else OPERATOR_ONLY
  return insert handoffPeers { principal }
mutation handoff(recipient: principal, body: text 1..200):
  require exists connected[recipient] else PEER_AWAY
  return insert durableHandoffs { sender: viewer, recipient, body }
query handoffs(c: cursor ?):
  return durableHandoffs where .sender = viewer first 20 by .id after c
query handoffInbox(c: cursor ?):
  return durableHandoffs where .recipient = viewer first 20 by .id after c
query handoffPresence(recipient: principal):
  return exists connected[recipient]
```

An operator enrolls recipients with a minted `service:ops` seat and
enrollHandoffPeer (`guide services`). Account signup alone grants no enrollment.
An open authenticated change poll counts as connected. After it ends, a
35-second grace bridges poll turnover and short disconnects. A handoff inside
that grace is intentionally accepted; after it expires it gets `PEER_AWAY`.
Reconnecting starts a new connected interval. Presence is a snapshot at the
server's decision, not an acknowledgement that a person saw the message, a
delivery guarantee, or a lock on their future connection. The row survives
disconnect and server restart. Use a recipient-written durable receipt for
acknowledgement. Test grace with `POST /dev/clock/advance` and
`{ "duration": "36s" }` after the peer's poll has ended.

## Scheduled publication, withdrawal and reapproval

`guide rule-recipes scheduled-reapproval` keeps editable drafts private and
copies the approved revision into an independent public table when due.
Editing or withdrawing atomically deletes the public copy and clears approval.
Reapproval is explicit, even for the same revision after withdrawal. The job
cannot republish a withdrawn revision. Scheduled runs coalesce; publication
happens at the next job run at or after publishAt, rather than at an exact
wall-clock instant (`guide jobs`).

```quarry
use personas alice, bob, carol
table scheduledDrafts:
  owner: principal
  body: text 1..200
  revision: int default 1
  approved: bool default false
  publishAt: time
  immutable owner
  read <- .owner = viewer
  insert <- .owner = viewer and .approved = false
  update <- .owner = viewer
  online only
table scheduledPublications:
  draft: scheduledDrafts
  revision: int
  body: text 1..200
  unique draft
  immutable draft
  public 'approved revision bodies are public until withdrawal'
  delete <- exists scheduledDrafts[.draft, owner = viewer]
  online only
fn retractScheduled(draft: scheduledDrafts):
  p = scheduledPublications[draft = draft]
  if p != null:
    delete scheduledPublications[p.id]
mutation newScheduledDraft(body: text 1..200, publishAt: time):
  return insert scheduledDrafts { owner: viewer, body, publishAt }
mutation editScheduledDraft(draft: scheduledDrafts, body: text 1..200):
  d = scheduledDrafts[draft] ! else NOT_FOUND
  retractScheduled(draft)
  update scheduledDrafts[draft] { body, revision: d.revision + 1, approved: false }
mutation approveScheduledDraft(draft: scheduledDrafts, publishAt: time):
  retractScheduled(draft)
  update scheduledDrafts[draft] { approved: true, publishAt }
mutation withdrawScheduledDraft(draft: scheduledDrafts):
  update scheduledDrafts[draft] { approved: false }
  retractScheduled(draft)
job publishScheduledDrafts(periodStart: time, periodEnd: time):
  for d in scheduledDrafts:
    if d.approved and d.publishAt <= periodEnd:
      upsert scheduledPublications[draft = d.id] { draft: d.id, revision: d.revision, body: d.body }
schedule scheduledPublisher every 1m: publishScheduledDrafts
query scheduledPublication(draft: scheduledDrafts):
  return scheduledPublications[draft = draft]
query scheduledDraft(draft: scheduledDrafts):
  return scheduledDrafts[draft]
```

Only the system job inserts/updates publications; the owner may delete a copy.
The approved bit always describes the current draft revision because the only
editing mutation clears it in the same transaction. Add that invariant to every
future draft writer. Already downloaded public content cannot be recalled.

## Private bookings with public availability counts

`guide rule-recipes booking-counts` discloses occupancy while keeping names
private. A maintained view sees all bookings; a caller-filtered count would
miss other people's reservations and oversell. Its public count deliberately
reveals even one-person join/leave activity. It is not anonymity against timing
inference. Empty authorized groups contribute zero.

```quarry
use personas alice, bob, carol
table availabilitySlots:
  capacity: int 1..20
  immutable capacity
  public 'slot capacity and occupied count are public'
  online only
table privateBookings:
  slot: availabilitySlots
  principal: principal
  unique slot, principal else ALREADY_BOOKED
  immutable slot, principal
  read <- .principal = viewer
  insert <- .principal = viewer
  delete <- .principal = viewer
  online only
view bookedCount: count privateBookings by .slot
  read <- allow
  disclosure reviewed 'public occupancy reveals reservation and cancellation deltas'
mutation reserveAvailable(slot: availabilitySlots):
  s = availabilitySlots[slot] ! else NOT_FOUND
  require bookedCount[slot] < s.capacity else SLOT_FULL
  return insert privateBookings { slot, principal: viewer }
mutation cancelAvailable(slot: availabilitySlots):
  delete privateBookings[slot, viewer]
query availability(slot: availabilitySlots):
  s = availabilitySlots[slot] ! else NOT_FOUND
  return { capacity: s.capacity, booked: bookedCount[slot], free: s.capacity - bookedCount[slot] }
query myBooking(slot: availabilitySlots):
  return privateBookings[slot, viewer]
```

Admission and insertion run in one writer transaction. Keep all booking writers
behind this capacity check, including future service paths. Capacity is
immutable so reducing it cannot silently invalidate existing bookings.

## Privacy-aware leaderboard

`guide rule-recipes private-leaderboard` publishes only an opted-in owner's
chosen display name and exact total. Attempts stay private. A maintained own
total includes every attempt; the only attempt writer refreshes an existing
public entry in the same transaction. Opting out deletes that entry. The
leaderboard cannot infer a nonparticipant's score by subtracting a global total.

```quarry
use personas alice, bob, carol
table privateAttempts:
  owner: principal
  points: int 0..100
  immutable owner, points
  read <- .owner = viewer
  insert <- .owner = viewer
  online only
view ownAttemptTotal: sum privateAttempts of .points by .owner
  read <- .owner = viewer
table publicLeaders:
  owner: principal
  name: text 1..40
  points: int
  unique owner
  immutable owner
  public 'opted-in display names, principals, exact totals and rank are public'
  insert <- .owner = viewer
  update <- .owner = viewer
  delete <- .owner = viewer
  online only
mutation recordAttempt(points: int 0..100):
  insert privateAttempts { owner: viewer, points }
  entry = publicLeaders[owner = viewer]
  if entry != null:
    update publicLeaders[entry.id] { points: ownAttemptTotal[viewer] }
mutation joinLeaderboard(name: text 1..40):
  return upsert publicLeaders[owner = viewer] { owner: viewer, name, points: ownAttemptTotal[viewer] }
mutation leaveLeaderboard():
  delete publicLeaders[owner = viewer]
query leaderboard(c: cursor ?):
  return publicLeaders last 20 by .points, .id after c { name, points }
query myAttemptTotal():
  return ownAttemptTotal[viewer]
```

This consent reveals score deltas and relative ordering, even in a small group;
principals remain public in the stored rows despite the query's projection.
Use a separate unlinked publication model if principal privacy is required.
Any future attempt edit/delete must refresh the public entry too. Never keep a
public aggregate over all private attempts as a shortcut.

## Mutable ancestor access across three levels

`guide rule-recipes three-level-live` uses one ordinary authority root at every
depth: project → folder → file. Folder and file each directly inherit the
project's whole live rule, with separate per-project product caps. The copied
project reference is immutable routing, not a copied mutable owner. The file
insert rule validates that its folder belongs to that project. Transferring the
project changes all existing folders and files atomically. No application
maintenance loop is required.

```quarry
use personas alice, bob, carol
table ancestorProjects:
  owner: principal
  read <- .owner = viewer or viewer = 'service:ops'
  update <- viewer = 'service:ops'
  online only
table ancestorFolders:
  project: ancestorProjects
  immutable project
  cap 5 by project
  read through live .project
  insert <- allow
  online only
table ancestorFiles:
  folder: ancestorFolders
  project: ancestorProjects
  body: text 1..100
  immutable folder, project
  cap 20 by project
  read through live .project
  insert <- exists ancestorFolders[.folder, project = .project]
  online only
mutation addAncestorFolder(project: ancestorProjects):
  return insert ancestorFolders { project }
mutation addAncestorFile(folder: ancestorFolders, body: text 1..100):
  f = ancestorFolders[folder] ! else NOT_FOUND
  return insert ancestorFiles { folder, project: f.project, body }
mutation transferAncestor(project: ancestorProjects, owner: principal):
  require viewer = 'service:ops' else OPERATOR_ONLY
  update ancestorProjects[project] { owner }
query ancestorFile(file: ancestorFiles):
  return ancestorFiles[file]
query ancestorFolder(folder: ancestorFolders):
  return ancestorFolders[folder]
```

LIVE's first form refuses chained `read through live .folder` when the folder
itself inherits a rule. Flatten the authority root as above when all levels
have identical readers. The 077 proposal workflow has additional per-section
owner grants; inheriting only its proposal would widen access. Its checks can
instead use `read through live .section`: the ordinary section's whole custom
rule includes a keyed probe of the current proposal state. This remains online;
the section itself must keep that custom rule rather than become a live child.
Keep copied routing keys still used by queries/writers and validate them on
insert. Bare captured `read through` cannot follow a mutable state flag. A mutable audience copy is
still appropriate for unsupported live compositions, such as timed parents,
or when the child intentionally has a different audience; maintain it
atomically through every parent writer (`current-roster`, Manual copy
alternative). An immutable audience copy instead preserves creation-time access.

The 085 hymn entries share their service's complete rule and can use
`read through live .service` online after removing the writing cascade action
from that endpoint; delete the capped entries explicitly before their service.
Its public-or-staff rule stays online. The full lane's cross-service hymnUsage
total still needs its existing explicit probe form: the planner cannot prove
that total uniform after direct LIVE conversion. A maintained view over the
live endpoint is also outside this first form's derivation boundary. Keeping
cascade semantics unchanged also requires the existing
explicit keyed probes. LIVE never silently
drops a reader branch or a reference action.

For the distinct per-section policy, keep that policy on the ordinary middle
table. This reduced 077 form retains the owner's active-only access and the
partner's signed-package access, with no authored ancestor copy on the check:

```quarry
use personas alice, bob, carol
table policyStaff:
  principal: principal
  role: enum('owner', 'partner')
  unique principal
  read <- .principal = viewer
  online only
table policyProposals:
  state: enum('active', 'signed') default 'active'
  read <- exists policyStaff[viewer]
  update <- exists policyStaff[viewer, role = 'partner']
  online only
table policySections:
  proposal: policyProposals
  owner: principal
  immutable proposal, owner
  read <- (.owner = viewer or exists policyStaff[viewer, role = 'partner']) and (exists policyProposals[.proposal, state = 'active'] or exists policyStaff[viewer, role = 'partner'])
  online only
table policyChecks:
  section: policySections
  body: text 1..80
  immutable section
  cap 20 by section
  read through live .section
  insert <- exists policyStaff[viewer, role = 'partner']
  online only
mutation addPolicyCheck(section: policySections, body: text 1..80):
  return insert policyChecks { section, body }
mutation signPolicyProposal(proposal: policyProposals):
  update policyProposals[proposal] { state: 'signed' }
query policyCheck(check: policyChecks):
  return policyChecks[check]
```

Changing a probed proposal state changes the online read verdict without
rewriting section/check rows. This differs from changing a policy input stored
on the immediate parent: LIVE maintains that input's private child copy in the
same parent transaction. Both keep the whole authored policy.

## Versioned status transitions with private history

`guide rule-recipes private-history` uses an expected version to reject a stale
transition, a named old/next rule for the permitted edge, and an append-only
owner-readable history row in the same transaction. No public status/history
aggregate leaks the private transition. A hidden task is `NOT_FOUND` before
the program's `STALE_VERSION` or `BAD_TRANSITION` checks.

```quarry
use personas alice, bob, carol
table versionedTasks:
  owner: principal
  status: enum('draft', 'submitted', 'approved') default 'draft'
  version: int default 1
  immutable owner
  read <- .owner = viewer
  insert <- .owner = viewer and .status = 'draft' and .version = 1
  update (old, next) <- old.owner = viewer and next.owner = old.owner and ((old.status = 'draft' and next.status = 'submitted') or (old.status = 'submitted' and next.status = 'approved'))
  online only
table privateStatusHistory:
  task: versionedTasks
  owner: principal
  version: int
  status: enum('draft', 'submitted', 'approved')
  at: time default now
  unique task, version
  immutable task, owner, version, status, at
  read <- .owner = viewer
  insert <- .owner = viewer and exists versionedTasks[.task, owner = .owner, version = .version, status = .status]
  online only
mutation newVersionedTask():
  return insert versionedTasks { owner: viewer }
mutation transitionTask(task: versionedTasks, expected: int, status: enum('draft', 'submitted', 'approved')):
  t = versionedTasks[task] ! else NOT_FOUND
  require t.version = expected else STALE_VERSION
  require (t.status = 'draft' and status = 'submitted') or (t.status = 'submitted' and status = 'approved') else BAD_TRANSITION
  update versionedTasks[task] { status, version: t.version + 1 }
  insert privateStatusHistory { task, owner: viewer, version: t.version + 1, status }
query versionedTask(task: versionedTasks):
  return versionedTasks[task]
query statusHistory(task: versionedTasks, c: cursor ?):
  return privateStatusHistory where .task = task and .owner = viewer first 20 by .version after c
```

The expected-version check and increment are application invariants in this
mutation; the table transition rule independently authorizes the status edge.
Keep every future writer on the same version/history path. History insert
failure rolls the status update back. `LANG4` owns the query-audience and
`N_REQUIRE_REFUSED` diagnostics for these workflows.
